be confining under
\alpha^{-1}
. Define a subset
\mathcal{L}(Q)
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
consisting of the set of all limits of convergent sequences
\gamma^{-k}x_{k}
where
x_{k}\in Q
for all
k\geq 0
. That is,

\mathcal{L}(Q)\vcentcolon=\left\{\lim_{k\to\infty}\gamma^{-k}x_{k}:\{x_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\subset Q\text{ and }\{\gamma^{-k}x_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\text{ is convergent}\right\}.

The following lemma gives an alternate characterization of
\mathcal{L}(Q)
.

Lemma 9.4
.

The set
\mathcal{L}(Q)
is an intersection of closures

\mathcal{L}(Q)=\bigcap_{k\geq 0}\overline{\gamma^{-k}(Q)}.

Proof.

For any
y\in\mathcal{L}(Q)
, there exist
x_{k}\in Q
such that
\gamma^{-k}x_{k}\to y
. Since
\gamma^{-k}(Q)
is closed under applying
\gamma^{-1}
, for any
l\geq k
we have
\gamma^{-l}x_{l}=\gamma^{-k}(\gamma^{-l+k}x_{l})\in\gamma^{-k}(Q)
. Thus
\{\gamma^{-l}x_{l}\}_{l\geq k}
is a sequence in
\gamma^{-k}(Q)
converging to
y
, and so
y\in\overline{\gamma^{-k}(Q)}
for each
k\geq 0
.

On the other hand, if
y\in\bigcap_{k\geq 0}\overline{\gamma^{-k}(Q)}
, then for each
k
there exists
\gamma^{-k}x_{k}\in\gamma^{-k}(Q)
with
d(y,\gamma^{-k}x_{k})\leq 1/k
(here distance is being measured in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
). Hence
\{x_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
is a sequence in
Q
and
\gamma^{-k}x_{k}\to y
, and so
y\in\mathcal{L}(Q)
.
∎

We now show that
\mathcal{L}(Q)
is invariant under
\gamma
.

Lemma 9.5
.

The set
\mathcal{L}(Q)
is a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that is invariant under
\gamma
.

Proof.

First we check that
\mathcal{L}(Q)
is an additive subgroup of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
y,z\in\mathcal{L}(Q)
, then we may choose sequences
\{x_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty},\{w_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\subset Q
so that
\gamma^{-k}x_{k}\to y
and
\gamma^{-k}w_{k}\to z
. Then
\gamma^{-k}(-x_{k})\to-y
, and since
-x_{k}\in Q
(recall
Q
is symmetric), we conclude that
-y\in\mathcal{L}(Q)
. Furthermore, choosing
N
large enough that
\gamma^{-N}(Q+Q)\subset Q
, we have
\gamma^{-N}(x_{k}+w_{k})\in Q
. Since
\gamma^{-k+N}(\gamma^{-N}(x_{k}+w_{k}))=\gamma^{-k}x_{k}+\gamma^{-k}w_{k}\to y+z
as
k\to\infty
, we see that
y+z\in\mathcal{L}(Q)
. Thus,
\mathcal{L}(Q)
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Since
\gamma^{-1}Q\subset Q
, it follows that
\gamma^{-1}\mathcal{L}(Q)=\mathcal{L}(\gamma^{-1}Q)\subset\mathcal{L}(Q)
. As an intersection of closed sets,
\mathcal{L}(Q)
is also closed. By a structure theorem of Bourbaki
[
11
, Section VII.1.2 Theorem 2]
, the closed subgroup
\mathcal{L}(Q)
contains a largest linear subspace
V
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{L}(Q)=V\oplus H
, where
H=\mathcal{L}(Q)\cap V^{\perp}
is discrete. Since
V
and
\gamma^{-1}V
are both subspaces of
\mathcal{L}(Q)
, we have
\gamma^{-1}V\subset V
. Since
V
and
\gamma^{-1}V
have the same dimension,
V=\gamma^{-1}V
. In particular,
V
is invariant under
\gamma
.

We claim that
H=0
, which will show that
\mathcal{L}(Q)=V
is an invariant subspace under
\gamma
.
The set
\mathcal{L}(Q)
consists of the disjoint translates
V+h
where
h
ranges over
H
. Since
\gamma^{-1}\mathcal{L}(Q)\subset\mathcal{L}(Q)
, the element
\gamma^{-1}
must send translates
V+h
to other such translates. If
H\neq 0
then we may choose
h\in H\setminus\{0\}
. It follows that the sets
\gamma^{-k}(V+h)
are translates of
V
which do not pass through the origin but accumulate at the origin. But this is impossible, since if
\epsilon
is small enough that the points in
H
are distance at least
\epsilon
apart, then
B_{\epsilon}(0)\cap\mathcal{L}(Q)
is contained in
V
. Thus
H=0
, completing the proof.
∎

Conversely we may associate confining subsets to invariant subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Throughout the remainder of this section,
\|M\|
will denote the operator norm of a matrix
M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the standard Euclidean inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\|v\|
will denote the Euclidean norm of a vector
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. As we are using an operator norm, we have
\|Mv\|\leq\|M\|\|v\|
for any
M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and any
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

Given an invariant subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we now define a confining subset of
A
as, essentially, the intersection of a neighborhood of this subspace with
A
, modulo some technical modifications.

Definition 9.6
.

Let
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a subspace that is invariant under
\gamma
. For any
\epsilon>0
, first define

Q_{\epsilon}^{0}(V)\vcentcolon=B_{\epsilon}(V)\cap A.

That is,
Q_{\epsilon}^{0}(V)
is the set of points of
A
in the open
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
V
. This set is not necessarily confining under
\alpha^{-1}
, because it may not be closed under applying
\alpha^{-1}
. To remedy this, choose
k_{0}
large enough so that
\|\gamma^{-k}\|<1
for all
k\geq k_{0}
, and define

Q_{\epsilon}(V)\vcentcolon=\bigcup_{0\leq k<k_{0}}\gamma^{-k}(Q_{\epsilon}^{0}(V)).

When
V
is understood we will frequently write simply
Q_{\epsilon}
.

Lemma 9.7
.

If
V
is a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that is invariant under
\gamma
, then
Q_{\epsilon}(V)\subseteq A
is confining under
\alpha^{-1}
.

Proof.

We first study properties of the set
Q_{\epsilon}^{0}(V)=Q_{\epsilon}^{0}
. It follows from the fact that
V
(and
A
) is invariant under
\gamma
and our choice of
k_{0}
that
\gamma^{-k}(Q_{\epsilon}^{0})\subset Q_{\epsilon}^{0}
for
k\geq k_{0}
. For any
x\in A
, we have
\|\gamma^{-k}x\|\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, and so
\gamma^{-k}(x)\in Q_{\epsilon}^{0}
for any sufficiently large
k
.
Choose
k_{1}
large enough that
\|\gamma^{-k_{1}}\|<\frac{1}{2}
; then
\gamma^{-k_{1}}(Q_{\epsilon}^{0}+Q_{\epsilon}^{0})\subset Q_{\epsilon}^{0}
.

With these properties in hand we can show that
Q_{\epsilon}
is confining. The fact that
Q_{\epsilon}
is closed under multiplication by
\gamma^{-1}
(Definition
2.10
(a)) follows from the following two facts:

(i)

\gamma^{-1}(\gamma^{-k}(Q_{\epsilon}^{0}))\subset\gamma^{-k-1}(Q_{\epsilon}^{0})
for
0\leq k<k_{0}-1
; and

(ii)

\gamma^{-1}(\gamma^{-k_{0}+1}(Q_{\epsilon}^{0}))\subset Q_{\epsilon}^{0}
.

Definition
2.10
(b) holds since it holds for
Q_{\epsilon}^{0}\subset Q_{\epsilon}
.
Finally, for any
y,z\in Q_{\epsilon}
we have
\gamma^{-k_{0}}(y),\gamma^{-k_{0}}(z)\in Q_{\epsilon}^{0}
. Thus,
\gamma^{-k_{0}-k_{1}}(y+z)\in Q_{\epsilon}^{0}\subset Q_{\epsilon}
, and Definition
2.10
(c) holds. Therefore,
Q_{\epsilon}
is confining under
\alpha^{-1}
.
∎

In the final results in this subsection, we investigate how the sets
Q_{\epsilon}(V)
change under modifying
\epsilon
or
V
. We start by showing that varying
\epsilon
yields equivalent confining subsets.

Lemma 9.8
.

If
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a subspace invariant under
\gamma
, then
Q_{\epsilon}(V)\sim Q_{\delta}(V)
for any
\epsilon,\delta>0
.

Proof.

Suppose without loss of generality that
\epsilon>\delta
. Then clearly
Q_{\epsilon}(V)\preceq Q_{\delta}(V)
. On the other hand, we may choose
n
large enough that
\|\gamma^{-n}\|<\delta/\epsilon
.
Thus
\gamma^{-n}(Q_{\epsilon}(V))\subset Q_{\delta}(V)
, and so
Q_{\delta}(V)\preceq Q_{\epsilon}(V)
.
∎

Now we investigate what happens to the confining subsets when we change subspaces. As expected, the order on confining subsets is reversed with respect to the inclusion of subspaces, and distinct subspaces yield inequivalent confining subsets.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
V,W\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be subspaces invariant under
\gamma
. If
V\subsetneq W
, then
Q_{\epsilon}(W)\precneq Q_{\epsilon}(V)
. Moreover, if neither
V\not\subset W
nor
W\not\subset V
, then
Q_{\epsilon}(V)
and
Q_{\epsilon}(W)
are incomparable.

Proof.

Since
A
contains
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, it is
\delta
-dense in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for any sufficiently large
\delta
. By Lemma
9.8
, we may choose
\epsilon=\delta
large enough to satisfy this property, without loss of generality. Suppose
V\subsetneq W
. Then
Q_{\epsilon}(V)\subset Q_{\epsilon}(W)
, and so
Q_{\epsilon}(W)\preceq Q_{\epsilon}(V)
. If
w\in Q_{\epsilon}(W)
, then the distance from
\gamma^{-r}w
to
V
is at least
\frac{1}{\|\gamma\|^{r}}
times the distance from
w
to
V
. Since
V\neq W
, we may choose elements of
W\subseteq Q_{\epsilon}(W)
at arbitrarily large distance from
V
.
This shows that
\inf\{k:\gamma^{-k}w\in Q_{\epsilon}(V)\}
is arbitrarily large for elements
w\in Q_{\epsilon}(W)
. Therefore,
Q_{\epsilon}(V)\not\preceq Q_{\epsilon}(W)
.

If
V\not\subset W
and
W\not\subset V
, then considering elements of
V
arbitrarily far from
W
shows that
Q_{\epsilon}(W)\not\preceq Q_{\epsilon}(V)
. Similarly,
Q_{\epsilon}(V)\not\preceq Q_{\epsilon}(W)
. This verifies the moreover statement.
∎

9.1
Tiles

Our goal is to show that all confining subsets of
A
under
\alpha^{-1}
are equivalent to the subsets
Q_{\epsilon}(V)
defined by subspaces
V
invariant under
\gamma
. In this subsection, we use
tiles
à la Lagarias–Wang
[
26
]
to show that if
Q
is such a confining subset, then
Q\preceq Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))
for some invariant subspace
V
(see Corollary
9.12
). The reverse relation will be shown in the next subsection.

We now choose a transversal
\mathscr{D}
for
\gamma\mathbb{Z}^{n}
in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and require that
0\in\mathscr{D}
. The cardinality of
\mathscr{D}
will be the absolute value of
\det\gamma
. Lagarias–Wang in
[
26
]
consider the following set:

Definition 9.10
.

A
tile
is a set

T=T(\gamma,\mathscr{D})=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{-j}v_{j}:v_{j}\in\mathscr{D}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\right\}.

Since
0\in\mathscr{D}
, the tile
T
contains the set of finite sums
\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{k}\gamma^{-j}v_{j}:k\geq 1\text{ and }v_{j}\in\mathscr{D}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\right\}\subset A
. As shown in
[
26
]
, the tile
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Lemma 9.11
.

If
Q\subset A
is confining under
\alpha^{-1}
, then for all
\epsilon>0
, there is a bound on the word length of any element of
B_{\epsilon}(0)\cap A
with respect to
Q\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}
.

Proof.

Fix any
\epsilon>0
, and let
z\in B_{\epsilon}(0)\cap A
. We may write
z=x+y
, where
x=\gamma^{-k}a_{-k}+\cdots+\gamma^{-1}a_{-1}
, for some
k\geq 1
and
a_{i}\in\mathscr{D}
, and
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. The element
x
is in
T
. Since
T
is compact and since
z=x+y
is lies in the bounded set
B_{\varepsilon}(0)\cap A
and the translate
T+y
, there are finitely many possibilities for the element
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. In particular, the word length of
y
is bounded.
By the method of proof of Lemma
6.3
, any finite sum
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma^{-i}v_{i}
with
v_{i}\in\mathscr{D}
has bounded word length in
Q\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}
(the proof is also more or less identical to that of
[
3
, Lemma 3.20]
). Hence
x
, and therefore also
z
, has bounded word length in
Q\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}
.
∎

Lemma 9.12
.

If
Q\subset A
is confining under
\alpha^{-1}
, then
Q\preceq Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))
for any
\epsilon>0
.

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon>0
. We will show that the set
Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))
has bounded word length with respect to
Q\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}
.
By Lemma
9.4
,
\mathcal{L}(Q)
lies in the closure of
Q
. Note that
Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))\subset Q_{\delta}^{0}(\mathcal{L}(Q))
for some suitably large
\delta\geq\epsilon
, where
Q_{\delta}^{0}(\mathcal{L}(Q))
is as in Definition
9.6
. So for any
z\in Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))
, there is
x\in\mathcal{L}(Q)
with
\|z-x\|<\delta
. By the definition of
\mathcal{L}(Q)
, there exists
y\in Q
with
\|x-y\|<\delta
. Thus

z\in B_{2\delta}(y)\cap A=y+(B_{2\delta}(0)\cap A)
. Lemma
9.11
provides a constant
D
large enough that the elements of
B_{2\delta}(0)\cap A
have word length at most
D
with respect to
Q\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}
. Therefore the word length of
z
with respect to
Q\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}
is at most
D+1
.
∎

The next lemma will be useful in the following subsections for proving that
Q\sim Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))
.

Lemma 9.13
.

There exist constants
A,B>0
and
0<\zeta<\eta<1
such that

A\zeta^{i}\leq\|\gamma^{-i}\|\leq B\eta^{i}

for all
i\geq 0
. Moreover, for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have
A\zeta^{i}\|v\|\leq\|\gamma^{-i}v\|\leq B\eta^{i}\|v\|
for all
i\geq 0
.

Proof.

Set
\zeta_{0}
and
\eta_{0}
to be the largest and smallest absolute value of an eigenvalue of
\gamma
, respectively. We have
\zeta_{0}\geq\eta_{0}>1
, since
\gamma
is expanding. By Gelfand’s formula, we have

\|\gamma^{i}\|^{1/i}\to\zeta_{0}\text{ and }\|\gamma^{-i}\|^{1/i}\to\eta_{0}^{-1}\text{ as }i\to\infty.

Choose any numbers
\zeta_{1},\eta_{1}
with
1<\zeta_{0}<\zeta_{1}
and
1>\eta_{1}>\eta_{0}^{-1}
. Then there exists
J>0
such that

\|\gamma^{i}\|^{1/i}<\zeta_{1}\text{ and }\|\gamma^{-i}\|^{1/i}<\eta_{1}\text{ for all }i>J.

Setting
A_{1}=\sup\{\|\gamma^{i}\|/\zeta_{1}^{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq J}
and
B_{1}=\sup\{\|\gamma^{-i}\|/\eta_{1}^{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq J}
, we immediately have that
\|\gamma^{i}\|\leq A_{1}\zeta_{1}^{i}
and
\|\gamma^{-i}\|\leq B_{1}\eta_{1}^{i}
for each
i\geq 0
. The first of these inequalities gives

1=\|\operatorname{Id}\|=\|\gamma^{-i}\gamma^{i}\|\leq\|\gamma^{-i}\|\|\gamma^{i}\|\leq\|\gamma^{-i}\|A_{1}\zeta_{1}^{i},

and so
\|\gamma^{-i}\|\geq\frac{1}{A_{1}}\zeta_{1}^{-i}
. Setting
A=A_{1}^{-1}
,
B=B_{1}
,
\zeta=\zeta_{1}^{-1}
, and
\eta=\eta_{1}
gives the claimed inequalities for
\|\gamma^{-i}\|
. For the moreover statement, the inequality
\|\gamma^{-i}v\|\leq B\eta^{i}\|v\|
follows immediately by definition of the operator norm. For the inequality
\|\gamma^{-i}v\|\geq A\zeta^{i}\|v\|
, note that
\|v\|=\|\gamma^{i}\gamma^{-i}v\|\leq A_{1}\zeta_{1}^{i}\|\gamma^{-i}v\|
.
∎

9.2
Invariant subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

In this subsection, we will complete the proof that
Q\sim Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))
by showing that
Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))\preceq Q
(Proposition
9.15
). To do so, we need to understand the structure of invariant subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We begin with a brief review of the generalized eigenspaces of
\gamma
and their properties.

The
generalized eigenspaces
of
\gamma
are the subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which we denote
C_{\lambda,i}
, defined by
C_{\lambda,i}\vcentcolon=\ker(\gamma-\lambda I)^{i}
, where
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is an eigenvalue of
\gamma
and
i\geq 0
. Each
C_{\lambda,i}
is invariant under
\gamma
. Moreover,

0=C_{\lambda,0}\subsetneq C_{\lambda,1}\subsetneq\ldots\subsetneq C_{\lambda,i_{\lambda}}=C_{\lambda,i_{\lambda}+1}=C_{\lambda,i_{\lambda}+2}=\ldots

for some unique number
i_{\lambda}
. The subspace
C_{\lambda,i_{\lambda}}
is the space of generalized
\lambda
-eigenvectors. Since we assume that the minimal and characteristic polynomials of
\gamma
are equal, we have in fact that
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}C_{\lambda,i}=i
for
0\leq i\leq i_{\lambda}
and
i_{\lambda}
is the algebraic multiplicity of
\lambda
.

If
\lambda
is real, then
\gamma
also preserves the real vector space
R_{\lambda,i}\vcentcolon=C_{\lambda,i}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}
for each
i
, and
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}R_{\lambda,i}=i
. In this case, there is an ordered basis
\mathcal{B}=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{i_{\lambda}}\}
for
R_{\lambda,i_{\lambda}}
which restricts to a basis
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{i}\}
of
R_{\lambda,i}
for each
i\leq i_{\lambda}
and such that
\gamma
acts on
R_{\lambda,i}
relative to this ordered basis
as the
i\times i
Jordan block with
\lambda
on the diagonal.

If
\lambda
is complex, then similarly there is an ordered basis
\mathcal{B}=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{i_{\lambda}}\}
for the complex vector space
C_{\lambda,i_{\lambda}}
which restricts to a basis
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{i}\}
for each
C_{\lambda,i}
and for which the action of
\gamma
on
C_{\lambda,i}
is given by an
i\times i
Jordan block as above. In this case, the vectors
w_{i}
are not real, and we consider the real and imaginary parts
u_{i}=\operatorname{Re}w_{i}
and
v_{i}=\operatorname{Im}w_{i}
. The element
\gamma
preserves each of the subspaces
R_{\lambda,i}\vcentcolon=\langle u_{1},v_{1},\ldots,u_{i},v_{i}\rangle
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
i=1,\ldots,i_{\lambda}
. Note that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}R_{\lambda,i}=2i
. Taking
\{u_{1},v_{1},\ldots,u_{i},v_{i}\}
as an ordered basis of
R_{\lambda,i}
and writing
\lambda=a+bi
, the matrix for
\gamma
relative to
\{u_{1},v_{1},\ldots,u_{i},v_{i}\}
is given by the
2i\times 2i

real Jordan block

\begin{pmatrix}Q(a,b)&I_{2}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&Q(a,b)&I_{2}&\cdots&0\\
0&0&Q(a,b)&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&Q(a,b)\end{pmatrix}

(3)

where
Q(a,b)
is the
2\times 2
matrix
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix}
and
I_{2}
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix.

If
\lambda
is not real, then we may consider the complex conjugate eigenvalue
\overline{\lambda}
. If
\mathcal{B}=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{i_{\lambda}}\}
is the ordered basis for
C_{\lambda,i_{\lambda}}
constructed in the last paragraph, then
\overline{\mathcal{B}}=\{\overline{w_{1}},\ldots,\overline{w_{i_{\lambda}}}\}
is an ordered basis for
C_{\overline{\lambda},i_{\lambda}}
satisfying all the same properties. We will assume that
\overline{\mathcal{B}}
has been chosen for
C_{\overline{\lambda},i_{\lambda}}
if
\mathcal{B}
has been chosen for
C_{\lambda,i}
. Then
R_{\lambda,i}=R_{\overline{\lambda},i}
for each
i
, and moreover,

(C_{\lambda,i}\oplus C_{\overline{\lambda},i})\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}=R_{\lambda,i}=R_{\overline{\lambda},i}.

The following is well-known (see
[
21
, Theorems 12.2.1 and 12.2.4]
).

Proposition 9.14
.

The subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that are invariant under
\gamma
are exactly the direct sums of subspaces of the form
R_{\lambda,i}
, where
\lambda
is a real or complex eigenvalue of
\gamma
and
0\leq i\leq i_{\lambda}
.

Lastly, we discuss real Jordan forms of matrices. Our discussion follows
[
19
, Section 3.1]
with certain modifications. A
real Jordan form
of
\gamma
is a real block diagonal matrix
\beta
with diagonal blocks that may be described as follows. Denote the real eigenvalues of
\gamma
by
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{u}
and the complex eigenvalues of
\gamma
(which occur in complex conjugate pairs) by
\mu_{1},\overline{\mu_{1}},\ldots,\mu_{s},\overline{\mu_{s}}
. Let
j_{t}
be the algebraic multiplicity of
\lambda_{t}
and
k_{t}
the algebraic multiplicity of
\mu_{t}
. Then
\beta
has one
j_{t}\times j_{t}
Jordan block for each real eigenvalue
\lambda_{t}
and one
2k_{t}\times 2k_{t}
real Jordan block for each complex conjugate pair
\mu_{t},\overline{\mu_{t}}
. The matrix
\gamma
is conjugate to
\beta
via a real invertible matrix. Moreover, the real Jordan form
\beta
is uniquely determined by
\gamma
up to permuting the diagonal blocks.

With this description of invariant subspaces and real Jordan forms in hand, we proceed to prove
Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))\preceq Q
, which, combined with Lemma
9.12
, will show that
Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))\sim Q
.

Proposition 9.15
.

If
Q\subset A
is confining under
\alpha^{-1}
and
\epsilon>0
, then
Q_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}(Q))\preceq Q
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we will write
V=\mathcal{L}(Q)
throughout the proof. By Lemma
9.8
,
Q_{\epsilon}(V)\sim Q_{\delta}(V)
for any
\epsilon,\delta>0
, so it suffices to prove the statement for
Q_{1}(V)
. If
Q
is contained in some metric neighborhood of
V
, then
Q_{1}(V)\preceq Q
by the proof of Lemma
9.8
.

Assume for contradiction that
Q
is not contained in
any
metric neighborhood of
V
. We will show that there is a compact set
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus V
that contains points of
\gamma^{-i}(Q)
for
i
arbitrarily large. Since
C
is compact, we may then take a subsequence to find points of
\gamma^{-i}(Q)
which converge to a point of
C
as
i\to\infty
. This will imply that that there is a point of
V
in
C
, which is a contradiction.

To prove the existence of such a set
C
, we consider the quotient vector space
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
. Since
V
is invariant,
\gamma
induces a linear automorphism
\overline{\gamma}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
. The real Jordan form for
\overline{\gamma}
may be obtained by taking sub-blocks of the real Jordan blocks of
\gamma
. This shows that the eigenvalues of
\overline{\gamma}
are a subset of the eigenvalues of
\gamma
, and hence

\overline{\gamma}
is also expanding.

The Euclidean norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
induces a norm
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
as follows. If
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\overline{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
is its equivalence class, then

\|\overline{u}\|^{\prime}=\|u-\operatorname{proj}_{V}(u)\|=d(u,V)

where
\operatorname{proj}_{V}
denotes orthogonal projection to
V
and
d(u,V)
denotes the distance from
u
to
V
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By Lemma
9.13
applied to the expanding matrix
\overline{\gamma}
, there are constants
A,B>0
and
0<\zeta<\eta<1
such that

A\zeta^{i}\|\overline{v}\|^{\prime}\leq\|\overline{\gamma}^{-i}\overline{v}\|^{\prime}\leq B\eta^{i}\|\overline{v}\|^{\prime}

for all
i\geq 0
and all
\overline{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
. Translating this into a statement about vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
yields that for all
i\geq 0
,

A\zeta^{i}d(v,V)\leq d(\gamma^{-i}v,V)\leq B\eta^{i}d(v,V).

By assumption, there are elements
v\in Q
with
d(v,V)
arbitrarily large. Since
d(\gamma^{-i}v,V)\geq A\zeta^{i}d(v,V)
, the quantity
\min\{i:\gamma^{-i}v\in Q_{1}^{0}(V)=B_{1}(V)\}
is arbitrarily large. Fix
v\in Q
, and set
j=\min\{i:\gamma^{-i}v\in Q_{1}^{0}(V)\}
. We may assume that
j>1
. By definition of
j
, we have
d(\gamma^{-j+1}v,V)>1
. Consequently,

1\geq d(\gamma^{-j}v,V)=d(\gamma^{-1}(\gamma^{-j+1}v),V)\geq A\zeta d(\gamma^{-j+1}v,V)\geq A\zeta.

Since
V
lies in the closure of
\gamma^{-j}(Q)
, we may choose
u\in Q
with
\gamma^{-j}(u)
arbitrarily close to
\operatorname{proj}_{V}(\gamma^{-j}v)
. Thus
\gamma^{-j}(v)-\gamma^{-j}(u)
may be taken to be nearly orthogonal to
V
and of length approximately
d(\gamma^{-j}(v),V)
. More precisely, we may choose
u\in Q
such that

\|\gamma^{-j}(v)-\gamma^{-j}(u)\|\leq 2\text{ and }d(\gamma^{-j}(v)-\gamma^{-j}(u),V)>A\zeta/2.

Consequently
\gamma^{-j}(v)-\gamma^{-j}(u)
lies in the compact set
C=\overline{B_{2}(0)}\setminus B_{A\zeta/2}(V)
. Since
\gamma^{-j}(v)-\gamma^{-j}(u)\in\gamma^{-j}(Q+Q)
, taking
k_{0}
large enough that
\gamma^{-k_{0}}(Q+Q)\subset Q
yields
\gamma^{-j}(v)-\gamma^{-j}(u)\in\gamma^{-j+k_{0}}(Q)
.

Since
j
may be taken arbitrarily large, we have shown that there are points of
\gamma^{-k}(Q)
in the compact set
C
for
k
arbitrarily large. Choose a sequence
k_{i}\to\infty
and
v_{i}\in Q
with
\gamma^{-k_{i}}(v_{i})\in C
. Since
C
is compact, we may pass to a subsequence to assume that
\gamma^{-k_{i}}(v_{i})
converges to a point
z\in C
. Then
\gamma^{-k_{i}}(v_{i})\in\gamma^{-k}(Q)
for any fixed
k
and for any
i
large enough, and hence
z
lies in the closure of
\gamma^{-k}(Q)
. Since
k
is arbitrary, this show that
z\in\mathcal{L}(Q)=V
by Lemma
9.4
. This is a contradiction, as
C\cap V=\emptyset
by construction.
∎

9.3
The poset of invariant subspaces

The subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
invariant under
\gamma
are partially ordered by inclusion. In this section we describe the resulting poset concretely. Let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}
be the real eigenvalues of
\gamma
and
\mu_{1},\overline{\mu_{1}},\ldots,\mu_{s},\overline{\mu_{s}}
the complex eigenvalues of
\gamma
. Let
k_{i}
be the algebraic multiplicity of
\lambda_{i}
and
l_{i}
the algebraic multiplicity of
\mu_{i}
. Then any invariant subspace has the form

R_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r},j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}=\left(\bigoplus_{u=1}^{r}R_{\lambda_{u},i_{u}}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{v=1}^{s}R_{\mu_{v},j_{v}}\right)

for some
i_{u}\leq k_{u}
and
j_{v}\leq l_{v}
for each
u
and
v
. We have
R_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r},j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}\subset R_{i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{r},j^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,j^{\prime}_{s}}
if and only if
i_{u}\leq i^{\prime}_{u}
and
j_{v}\leq j^{\prime}_{v}
for each
u
and
v
. Thus the following proposition follows immediately:

Proposition 9.16
.

Let
k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}
be the algebraic multiplicities of the real eigenvalues of
\gamma
. Choose one eigenvalue from each complex conjugate pair of complex eigenvalues of
\gamma
, and let
l_{1},\ldots,l_{s}
be their algebraic multiplicities. The poset of subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
invariant under
\gamma
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Div}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r},l_{1},\ldots,l_{s})
.

We are now ready to prove Proposition
9.1
.

Proof of Proposition
9.1
.

Consider an expanding matrix
\gamma\in M_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
with equal minimal and characteristic polynomial, and form the group
G=G(\gamma)
. The lattice
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
is isomorphic to the poset of confining subsets of
A
under
\alpha^{-1}
, which is multiplication by
\gamma^{-1}
, by Proposition
4.7
. If
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an invariant subspace for
\gamma
then the set
Q_{1}(V)
is confining. Thus there is a function
V\mapsto Q_{1}(V)
from invariant subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to confining subsets of
A
considered up to equivalence. By Lemma
9.9
this function is injective and order-reversing. By Lemmas
9.12
and
9.15
this function is surjective: if
Q\subset A
is confining then
\mathcal{L}(Q)
is an invariant subspace by Lemma
9.5
and we have
Q\sim Q_{1}(\mathcal{L}(Q))
. Therefore,
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
is isomorphic to the opposite of the poset of invariant subspaces of
\gamma
. By Proposition
9.16
, this poset is isomorphic to its own opposite, so
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
is isomorphic to the poset of invariant subspaces.
∎

9.4
Corollaries to Theorem
1.2

Having discussed invariant subspaces, we will now pause to derive the various corollaries to Theorem
1.2
. We do this to illustrate the immediate applications of the invariant subspace machinery. The only component of Theorem
1.2
that remains to be proven is the association to actions on Heintze groups; this will be completed in Section
10
.

Proof of Corollary
1.6
.

Consider the monic polynomial
p(x)=a_{0}+a_{1}x+\cdots+a_{n-1}x^{n-1}+x^{n}
satisfying the hypotheses of the corollary and the associated ring
R=\mathbb{Z}[x]/(p)
. Then
R
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
as an abelian group, with
\{1+(p),x+(p),\ldots,x^{n-1}+(p)\}
being a basis of free abelian groups. Of course,
R
is itself a cyclic
R
-module. Moreover, with respect to our free basis for
R
, the matrix of the linear endomorphism of
R
defined by multiplication by
\gamma=x+(p)
is exactly the companion matrix
\delta
to
p
. The matrix
\delta
is expanding since its characteristic polynomial (and minimal polynomial) is
p
, all of whose roots lie outside the unit disk. Hence
G(R,\gamma)
is isomorphic to
G(\delta)=\langle\mathbb{Z}^{n},t:trt^{-1}=\delta r\text{ for }r\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\rangle
and the poset
\mathcal{P}_{+}(G(R,\gamma))
is described by Theorem
1.2
. Thus,
\mathcal{P}_{+}(G)
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Div}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{r})
, where
p=up_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots p_{r}^{n_{r}}
is the prime factorization of
p
in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
.

It remains only to describe
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
, the poset of invariant subspaces for
\delta
. Since
p
is irreducible, it has no repeated roots in
\mathbb{C}
. By Proposition
9.16
, the poset of invariant subspaces is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Div}(1,\ldots,1)
with the number of 1’s between the parentheses being the number of real roots plus half the number of complex roots of
p
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.7
.

The statement about
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
follows from Corollary
1.6
. It remains to describe
\mathcal{P}_{+}(G)
, which is isomorphic to the poset of divisors of
p
in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
, up to associates. Let
(-1)^{s}q_{1}\cdots q_{r}
be the prime factorization of the constant term of
p
. Then by Corollary
5.10
,
p
factors as
p=(-1)^{s}p_{1}\cdots p_{r}
where the constant term of
p_{i}
is
q_{i}
. By Lemma
5.14
,
p_{i}
is a prime power series. Hence
p=(-1)^{s}p_{1}\cdots p_{r}
is the prime factorization of
p
, and so the poset
\mathcal{P}_{+}(G)
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Div}(1,\ldots,1)
where the number of 1’s between the parentheses is
r
, the number of prime factors of the constant term of
p
.
∎

10
Elements of
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
: actions on Heintze groups

The goal of this section is to prove the remaining part of Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.8
, that each element of
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
contains an action on a Heintze group. The theorems will follow from:

Theorem 10.1
.

Fix an expanding matrix
\gamma\in M_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
whose characteristic and minimal polynomials are equal, and consider the ascending HNN extension
G=G(\gamma)
defined by
\gamma
.
Every element of
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
contains an action of
G
on a quasi-convex subspace
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}
of a Heintze group
\mathbb{C}^{k}\rtimes\mathbb{R}
for some
k\leq n
.

Recall that
A
is the
\mathbb{Z}[\gamma]
-module
A=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\gamma^{-i}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
(see Definition
9.2
), a sub-module of
\mathbb{Q}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and that
A
is identified with the normal closure of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
in
G(\gamma)
.

Our strategy for proving Theorem
10.1
is to embed
G=A\rtimes\mathbb{Z}
into a
Heintze group

\mathbb{C}^{n}\rtimes\mathbb{R}
. This Heintze group can be equipped with a left-invariant Riemannian metric that makes it a hyperbolic metric space on which
G
acts on by isometries (details are provided below). However, this action will not be cobounded and, in particular, will not define an element of
\mathcal{H}(G)
. To deal with this problem, we single out an embedded quasi-convex subspace of
\mathbb{C}^{n}\rtimes\mathbb{R}
on which
G
acts coboundedly. Quasiconvexity of the subspace ensures that it is also a hyperbolic metric space, and so the action on this subspace
will
define an element of
\mathcal{H}(G)
.

To form a Heintze group, we need a one-parameter subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
containing
\gamma
. We will denote such a subgroup by
\gamma^{t}=e^{tX}
where
X
lies in
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, the Lie algebra of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
consisting of all
n\times n
complex matrices. However, we emphasize that the matrix
\gamma
lies on multiple one-parameter subgroups, so that
\gamma^{t}
is not defined solely in terms of
\gamma
. For ease in the proofs that follow, we will choose the one-parameter subgroup carefully as in the following lemma, which we prove later in this section.

Lemma 10.2
.

The matrix
\gamma
lies on a one-parameter subgroup
\gamma^{t}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
satisfying
\gamma^{t}=\delta^{t}\epsilon^{t}
, where

•

the one-parameter subgroup
\delta^{t}
is conjugate into
U(n)
, the group of unitary
n\times n
matrices;

•

the one-parameter subgroup
\epsilon^{t}
is contained in
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
; and

•

the groups
\delta^{t}
and
\epsilon^{t}
commute.

In the special case that
\gamma
lies on a one-parameter subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, we may also define a Heintze group
\mathbb{R}^{n}\rtimes\mathbb{R}
into which
G
embeds. It will carry a left-invariant hyperbolic metric. The action of
G
will, in fact, be cobounded in this case, and hence it will not be necessary to pass to a subspace. However, in the case that
\gamma
does not lie on a one-parameter subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, we will need the full generality of the following discussion.

Consider
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the Euclidean norm
\|\cdot\|
. This is preserved by the unitary matrices
U(n)
. We let
\|\cdot\|
denote the induced operator norm on
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

Corollary 10.3
.

Let
\gamma^{t}=\delta^{t}\epsilon^{t}
be a one-parameter subgroup as in Lemma
10.2
. There is a constant
M\geq 1
such that
\|\delta^{t}\|\leq M
for all
t
.

Proof.

Choose
\xi\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
with
\xi\delta^{t}\xi^{-1}\in U(n)
for all
t
. Then
\|\delta^{t}\|\leq\|\xi^{-1}\|\|\xi\delta^{t}\xi^{-1}\|\|\xi\|=\|\xi^{-1}\|\|\xi\|
, and we set
M=\|\xi^{-1}\|\|\xi\|
. Note that
M\geq\|\xi^{-1}\xi\|=1
.
∎

With this particular one-parameter subgroup in hand, we let
\mathbb{R}
act on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by the group
\gamma^{t}
and define the group
H=\mathbb{C}^{n}\rtimes_{\gamma^{t}}\mathbb{R}
. That is,
H
is identified with
\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
as a set and the group operation is given by
(z,t)\cdot(w,s)=(z+\gamma^{t}w,t+s)
. The Lie group
H
is an example of a Heintze group (see
[
23
]
for the general definition) and is equipped with a left-invariant Riemannian metric defined as follows. As a manifold,
H
may be equipped with a single chart
(z,t)\mapsto(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},t)
if
z=(x_{1}+iy_{1},\ldots,x_{n}+iy_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}.
The vector fields
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}},\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}
, and
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
identify each tangent space of
H
with
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
in the natural way. At the identity
(0,0)
, we equip
H
with the usual Euclidean metric
dx_{1}^{2}+dy_{1}^{2}+\cdots+dx_{n}^{2}+dy_{n}^{2}+dt^{2}.

The inner product on
T_{(z,t)}(H)
is given by pulling back the metric at
(0,0)
via left-translations, i.e., the inner product of
(v,r),(w,s)\in T_{(z,t)}(H)\cong\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
is
\langle\gamma^{-t}v,\gamma^{-t}w\rangle+rs
.

Theorem 10.4

(
[
23
, Theorem 3]
)
.

The Lie group
H
with the left-invariant Riemannian metric described above has negative sectional curvature.

Since
H
has a cocompact group of isometries (i.e., its action on itself by left translations),
H
in fact has sectional curvature
\leq\kappa
for some
\kappa<0
. Thus
H
is a complete, locally CAT(
\kappa
) space. It follows from the Cartan-Hadamard Theorem
[
14
, Theorem II.4.1]
that
H
is in fact globally CAT(
\kappa
):

Corollary 10.5
.

The Lie group
H
is CAT(
\kappa
) for some
\kappa<0
and therefore Gromov hyperbolic.

The group
G=A\rtimes\mathbb{Z}
naturally embeds into
H=\mathbb{C}^{n}\rtimes\mathbb{R}
and therefore acts isometrically on
H
by left translations. However, this action is never cobounded. Instead, we see that
G
preserves the subspace
P=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
of the space
H=\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
. It is important to emphasize that
P
is
not
a subgroup of
H
(unless
\gamma^{t}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
) but simply a sub-manifold. We equip
P
with the induced Riemannian metric as a sub-manifold of
H
. However, the submanifold
P
still admits a useful structure proved in the next result.

Proposition 10.6
.

The subspace
P
is quasi-isometrically embedded and hence quasiconvex in
H
.

Proof.

There is a retraction
\rho\colon H\to P
defined by
\rho(z,t)=(\operatorname{Re}z,t)
. It suffices to show that
\rho
is a Lipschitz map.
For this, it suffices in turn to show that the derivative
\rho_{*}
of
\rho
is uniformly Lipschitz on tangent spaces.

Consider a point
(z,t)\in H
and a tangent vector
(v,s)\in T_{(z,t)}(H)
. The map
\rho
sends
(z,t)
to
(\operatorname{Re}z,t)
and
(v,s)
to
(\operatorname{Re}v,s)\in T_{(\operatorname{Re}z,t)}(H)
. We have

\|(v,s)\|^{2}=\|\gamma^{-t}v\|^{2}+s^{2}=\|\delta^{-t}\epsilon^{-t}v\|^{2}+s^{2}.

By Corollary
10.3
, we have
\|\delta^{-t}\epsilon^{-t}v\|\geq\frac{1}{\|\delta^{t}\|}\|\epsilon^{-t}v\|\geq\frac{1}{M}\|\epsilon^{-t}v\|
. Thus

\|(v,s)\|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{M^{2}}\|\epsilon^{-t}v\|^{2}+\frac{1}{M^{2}}s^{2}.

On the other hand,
\|(\operatorname{Re}v,s)\|^{2}=\|\delta^{-t}\epsilon^{-t}\operatorname{Re}v\|^{2}+s^{2}
. Since
\epsilon^{-t}
is real, we have
\epsilon^{-t}\operatorname{Re}v=\operatorname{Re}\epsilon^{-t}v
, and thus,

\|(\operatorname{Re}v,s)\|^{2}=\|\delta^{-t}\operatorname{Re}\epsilon^{-t}v\|^{2}+s^{2}\leq M^{2}\|\operatorname{Re}\epsilon^{-t}v\|^{2}+M^{2}s^{2}\leq M^{2}\|\epsilon^{-t}v\|^{2}+M^{2}s^{2}.

Therefore
\rho_{*}
and
\rho
are
M^{2}
-Lipschitz.
∎

Lastly, it remains to prove Lemma
10.2
in order to proceed to the proof of Theorem
10.1
. For this we recall the real Jordan form of the matrix
\gamma
. To describe it, consider as before the real and complex eigenvalues of
\gamma
. We further partition the real eigenvalues of
\gamma
into a set
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{u}
of positive eigenvalues and a set
-\nu_{1},\ldots,-\nu_{v}
of negative eigenvalues. As before, denote the complex eigenvalues by
\mu_{1},\overline{\mu_{1}},\ldots,\mu_{s},\overline{\mu_{s}}
, and set
\mu_{j}=a_{j}+b_{j}i
with
a_{j},b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
. For a real number
\lambda
, denote by
J(\lambda,k)
the
k\times k
Jordan block with
\lambda
on the diagonal. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
denote by
M(a,b,k)
the
2k\times 2k
real Jordan block defined in (
3
). Denote by
j_{t},k_{t},
and
l_{t}
the algebraic multiplicities of
\lambda_{t},\nu_{t},
and
\mu_{t}
, respectively. Then there is a real invertible matrix
\eta
such that
\eta\gamma\eta^{-1}
is the real Jordan form of
\gamma
; i.e.,
\eta\gamma\eta^{-1}
is block diagonal with diagonal blocks
J(\lambda_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,J(\lambda_{u},j_{u}),J(-\nu_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,J(-\nu_{v},k_{v}),M(a_{1},b_{1},l_{1}),\ldots,M(a_{s},b_{s},l_{s})
occurring in that order.

Our discussion of one-parameter groups is related to the discussion in
[
19
, Section 3.1]
.

Proof of Lemma
10.2
.

To prove the lemma, we construct one-parameter groups of matrices containing each block of the real Jordan form of
\gamma
. We will then put these all together to create a one-parameter subgroup of the desired form containing
\gamma
. We consider each type of block in turn.

Case 1:
J(\lambda,k)
,
\lambda>0
.
Consider a Jordan block
J(\lambda,k)
for
\lambda
a positive real number and
k>0
. We will show that
J(\lambda,k)
lies on a one-parameter subgroup
\mathcal{G}(\lambda,k,t)
such that
\mathcal{G}(\lambda,k,1)=J(\lambda,k)
.

Consider the
k\times k
nilpotent matrix
N
with 1’s on the superdiagonal and zeros elsewhere. The matrix exponential
e^{tN}
is seen to be

\begin{pmatrix}1&t&\frac{1}{2!}t^{2}&\cdots&\frac{1}{(k-2)!}t^{k-2}&\frac{1}{(k-1)!}t^{k-1}\\
0&1&t&\cdots&\frac{1}{(k-3)!}t^{k-3}&\frac{1}{(k-2)!}t^{k-2}\\
0&0&1&\cdots&\frac{1}{(k-4)!}t^{k-4}&\frac{1}{(k-3)!}t^{k-3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1&t\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

In particular, consider what happens when
t=1
. The matrices

e^{N}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&\frac{1}{2!}&\cdots&\frac{1}{(k-2)!}&\frac{1}{(k-1)!}\\
0&1&1&\cdots&\frac{1}{(k-3)!}&\frac{1}{(k-2)!}\\
0&0&1&\cdots&\frac{1}{(k-4)!}&\frac{1}{(k-3)!}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1&1\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0&\cdots&0&0\\
0&1&1&\cdots&0&0\\
0&0&1&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1&1\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&\lambda^{-1}&0&\cdots&0&0\\
0&1&\lambda^{-1}&\cdots&0&0\\
0&0&1&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1&\lambda^{-1}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&1\end{pmatrix}

are all conjugate in
\operatorname{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{R})
, as can be seen by considering the dimensions of their generalized eigenspaces for the unique eigenvalue 1. The matrix in the middle is the Jordan normal form of all three. Thus, choosing
\theta\in\operatorname{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{R})
conjugating the first matrix to the last, we have that
J(\lambda,k)=\lambda^{1}\theta e^{N}\theta^{-1}
. In particular,
J(\lambda,k)
lies on the one-parameter subgroup
\mathcal{G}(\lambda,k,t)\vcentcolon=\lambda^{t}(\theta e^{Nt}\theta^{-1})
of
\operatorname{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{R})
(note that
\lambda^{t}
here is just a scalar, which commutes with any matrix
\theta e^{Nt^{\prime}}\theta^{-1}
).

Case 2:
J(-\nu,k)
,
\nu>0
.
Next we consider a Jordan block
J(-\nu,k)
where
\nu
is a positive real number. As in the last paragraph, there is a one-parameter subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{R})
containing
-J(-\nu,k)
. Let
\mathcal{H}(-\nu,k,t)\vcentcolon=e^{tA}
with
A\in M_{k}(\mathbb{R})
be such a subgroup, so that
e^{A}=-J(-\nu,k)
. Note that
e^{tA}
commutes with the scalars
e^{i\pi t^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}
and
J(-\nu,k)=e^{i\pi}e^{A}
. Thus
e^{i\pi t}\mathcal{H}(-\nu,k,t)=e^{i\pi t}e^{tA}
is a one-parameter subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{C})
(not
\operatorname{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{R})
) which contains
J(-\nu,k)
.

Case 3:
M(a,b,k)
.
Finally, consider a matrix
M(a,b,k)
where
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
are not both zero, and let
r=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}>0
. We first consider the matrix
Q(a,b)
, which can be written as
rQ(a/r,b/r)
where
Q(a/r,b/r)
is a rotation matrix. Consequently,
Q(a/r,b/r)
lies on the one-parameter subgroup
e^{tK}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, where
K
is the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
. Replace the matrix
K\in M_{2}(\mathbb{R})
by a scalar multiple to ensure that
Q(a/r,b/r)=e^{K}
. Then
Q(a,b)
lies on the one-parameter subgroup
r^{t}e^{tK}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Note that
e^{tK}
commutes with the scalars
r^{t^{\prime}}
and all the matrices
e^{tK}
are orthogonal (and, in particular, unitary).

Now consider the full matrix
M(a,b,k)
. The nilpotent matrix
N\in M_{2k}(\mathbb{R})
defined by

N=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{2}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&I_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&I_{2}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}\text{ satisfies }e^{tN}=\begin{pmatrix}I&tI&\frac{1}{2!}t^{2}I&\cdots&\frac{1}{(k-1)!}t^{k-1}I\\
0&I&tI&\cdots&\frac{1}{(k-2)!}t^{k-2}I\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&tI\\
0&0&0&\cdots&I\end{pmatrix}

(where all blocks in these matrices are
2\times 2
). As before, we see that
e^{N}
is conjugate via a real invertible matrix to the real Jordan block

\begin{pmatrix}I&I&0&\cdots&0\\
0&I&I&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&I\\
0&0&0&\cdots&I\end{pmatrix}.

In fact, if
(a_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{k}
is any matrix conjugating the first matrix to the second in equation (
10
), then the same matrix with each entry
a_{ij}
replaced by the
2\times 2
block
a_{ij}I
conjugates
e^{N}
to the desired
2k\times 2k
real Jordan block. Denote this conjugating block matrix by
\xi
. Our
2k\times 2k
real Jordan block is in turn conjugate to

\begin{pmatrix}I&Q(a,b)^{-1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&I&Q(a,b)^{-1}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&Q(a,b)^{-1}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&I\end{pmatrix}\text{ via }\zeta=\begin{pmatrix}Q(a,b)^{-k+1}&&&&\\
&Q(a,b)^{-k+2}&&&\\
&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&\\
&&&&Q(a,b)^{0}\end{pmatrix}.

We have shown that
\zeta\xi
conjugates
e^{N}
to the first matrix in (
10
). Define
\mathcal{V}(a,b,k,t)\vcentcolon=r^{t}\zeta\xi e^{tN}\xi^{-1}\zeta^{-1}
, and let
\mathcal{U}(a,b,k,t)
be the
2k\times 2k
block diagonal matrix with diagonal blocks
e^{tK}
. Thus,
M(a,b,k)
lies on the one-parameter subgroup
\mathcal{W}(a,b,k,t)\vcentcolon=\mathcal{U}(a,b,k,t)\mathcal{V}(a,b,k,t)
.
The matrix
\mathcal{U}(a,b,k,t)
is orthogonal (in particular unitary),
\mathcal{V}(a,b,k,t)
is real, and moreover,
\mathcal{U}(a,b,k,t)
commutes with
\mathcal{V}(a,b,k,t^{\prime})
for any
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. To see this last fact, note that
\mathcal{U}(a,b,k,t)
commutes with:
r^{t^{\prime}}
since it’s a scalar;
\zeta
, since
Q(a,b)
and
e^{tK}
lie in the same one-parameter subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
up to scaling; and
\xi
and
e^{t^{\prime}N}
since
\mathcal{U}(a,b,k,t)
is block-diagonal and
\xi
and
e^{t^{\prime}N}
are block matrices whose blocks are scalar multiples of
I_{2}
.

We now show how to combine these one-parameter groups to form the desired one-parameter subgroup containing
\gamma
. Recall the positive real eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{u}
, the negative real eigenvalues
-\nu_{1},\ldots,-\nu_{v}
, and the complex eigenvalues
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s}
with
\mu_{j}=a_{j}+ib_{j}
. Denote by
\mathcal{G}(t)
the block diagonal matrix with diagonal blocks
\mathcal{G}(\lambda_{1},j_{1},t),\ldots,\mathcal{G}(\lambda_{u},j_{u},t)
. Denote by
\mathcal{I}(t)
the block diagonal matrix with diagonal blocks
e^{i\pi t}\mathcal{H}(-\nu_{1},k_{1},t),\ldots,e^{i\pi t}\mathcal{H}(-\nu_{v},k_{v},t)
. The matrix
\mathcal{I}(t)
is the product of
e^{i\pi t}
with
\mathcal{H}(t)
, where
\mathcal{H}(t)
is the block diagonal matrix with diagonal blocks
\mathcal{H}(-\nu_{j},k_{j},t)
. Finally denote by
\mathcal{W}(t)
the block diagonal matrix with diagonal blocks
\mathcal{W}(a_{1},b_{1},l_{1},t),\ldots,\mathcal{W}(a_{s},b_{s},l_{s},t)
. We have
\mathcal{W}(a_{j},b_{j},l_{j},t)=\mathcal{U}(a_{j},b_{j},l_{j},t)\mathcal{V}(a_{j},b_{j},l_{j},t)
for each
j
, and thus
\mathcal{W}(t)=\mathcal{U}(t)\mathcal{V}(t)
where
\mathcal{U}(t)
is block diagonal with diagonal blocks
\mathcal{U}(a_{j},b_{j},l_{j},t)
and
\mathcal{V}(t)
is block diagonal with diagonal blocks
\mathcal{V}(a_{j},b_{j},l_{j},t)
. Combining the three cases considered above, we see that
\eta\gamma\eta^{-1}
, the real Jordan form of
\gamma
, lies on the one-parameter subgroup

\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{G}(t)&&\\
&\mathcal{I}(t)&\\
&&\mathcal{W}(t)\end{pmatrix}.

Setting
J=j_{1}+\cdots+j_{u}
and
K=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{v}
, we see that this is the product of two commuting one-parameter subgroups

\begin{pmatrix}I_{J}&&\\
&e^{i\pi t}I_{K}&\\
&&\mathcal{U}(t)\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{G}(t)&&\\
&\mathcal{H}(t)&\\
&&\mathcal{V}(t)\end{pmatrix}.

The first of these two subgroups is a unitary subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and the second is a subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
. Finally, set
\delta^{t}
to be the conjugate of the (first) unitary one-parameter group by
\eta^{-1}
and
\epsilon^{t}
to be the conjugate of the (second) real one-parameter group by
\eta^{-1}
. Since
\eta
is a real invertible matrix,
\delta^{t}
and
\epsilon^{t}
are subgroups of the form specified by the lemma.
∎

10.1
The word metric for the main Heintze group

In this section we study the action of
G=A\rtimes\mathbb{Z}
on the quasi-convex subspace
P=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
of the Heintze group
\mathbb{C}^{n}\rtimes_{\gamma^{t}}\mathbb{R}
. We will show that the action of
G
on
P
is cobounded (Lemma
10.7
) and that the action
G\curvearrowright P
is equivalent to the action
G\curvearrowright\Gamma(G,Q_{\epsilon}(0)\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\})
(Lemma
10.8
). This will show that the quasi-parabolic structure determined by the invariant subspace 0 is represented by the action on
P
. In the next subsection we investigate the quasi-parabolic structures corresponding to other invariant subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and show they correspond to actions of
G
on subspaces of quotients of the Heintze group
\mathbb{C}^{n}\rtimes\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 10.7
.

The action
G\curvearrowright P
is cobounded.

Proof.

We first claim that the orbit of the base point
(0,0)\in P
under the action of
A
is dense in the subset
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{0\}
of
P
. To see this, note that
A
contains the set

\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{k}\gamma^{-j}v_{j}:k\geq 1\text{ and }v_{j}\in\mathscr{D}\text{ for all }1\leq j\leq k\right\},

which is dense in the tile

T=T(\gamma,\mathscr{D})=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{-j}v_{j}:v_{j}\in\mathscr{D}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\right\}.

By
[
26
, Corollary 1.1]
the tile
T
contains an open set. Hence
A
is dense in some open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since the closed subgroup
\overline{A}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
contains an open set of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, Bourbaki’s structure theorem
[
11
, Chapter 7, Theorem 2]
implies that it must in fact be equal to all of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Therefore,
A
is dense.

Now, every point of
P
lies at distance
\leq 1
from a horosphere
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{m\}
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
t^{m}
translates
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{0\}
to this horosphere, we see that the orbit of
(0,0)
under
G
is dense in this horosphere as well. Choosing any
\epsilon>0
, we see that every point of
P
is distance
\leq 1+\epsilon
from the orbit of
(0,0)
.
∎

Lemma 10.8
.

We have
(G\curvearrowright P)\sim(G\curvearrowright\Gamma(G,Q_{\epsilon}(0)\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}))
for any
\epsilon>0
.

Proof.

Since the orbit of the base point
b=(0,0)
is
2
-dense in
P
, it follows from the Schwarz–Milnor Lemma (Lemma
2.6
) that
G\curvearrowright P
is equivalent to
G\curvearrowright\Gamma(G,S)
, where
S=\{g\in G:d(b,gb)\leq 5\}
.

Any element
g\in S\subset G
can be written as
g=xt^{j}
with
x\in A
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that
gb=(x,j)
. Consider a geodesic
p\colon[0,1]\to P
from
b
to
gb
, where
p(u)=(z(u),s(u))
for
0\leq u\leq 1
. Since every point on the horosphere
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{j\}
is distance at least
|j|
from
b
and the length of
p
is at most 5, we must have
|s(u)|\leq 5
for all
0\leq u\leq 1
. Choose
L\geq 1
with
\frac{1}{L}\leq\|\gamma^{s}\|\leq L
for all
s
with
|s|\leq 5
. Then we have (denoting derivatives by
\cdot^{\prime}
),

d(b,gb)=\ell(p)=\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\|\gamma^{-s(u)}z^{\prime}(u)\|^{2}+s^{\prime}(u)^{2}}du\geq\frac{1}{L}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\|z^{\prime}(u)\|^{2}+s^{\prime}(u)^{2}}du.

This lower bound is
\frac{1}{L}
times the length of
p
when considered as a path in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
with the Euclidean metric. Thus,

5\geq d(b,gb)\geq\frac{1}{L}\sqrt{\|x\|^{2}+j^{2}},

and we have both
\|x\|\leq 5L
and
|j|\leq 5L
. In other words,
Q_{5L}(0)\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}\preceq S
.

On the other hand, we have
t\in S
. Furthermore, the norm of any element of
Q_{\epsilon}(0)
is uniformly bounded in terms of
\epsilon
, and this uniform bound approaches 0 as
\epsilon\to 0
. Choose
\eta
small enough that every element of
Q_{\eta}(0)
has norm bounded by 5. For any
x\in Q_{\eta}(0)
, the (Euclidean) straight line path
p
from
b=(0,0)
to
xb=(x,0)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{0\}
has length equal to its Euclidean length. Hence
d(b,xb)\leq\|x\|\leq 5
, so that
x\in S
as well. This shows that
S\preceq Q_{\eta}(0)\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}
, and the result now follows from Lemma
9.8
, as
Q_{\eta}(0)\sim Q_{5L}(0)
.
∎

10.2
Quotients of Heintze groups

The previous section showed that the quasi-parabolic structure corresponding to the invariant subspace
0
is represented by the action on the quasiconvex subspace
P=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
of the Heintze group
\mathbb{C}^{n}\rtimes_{\gamma^{t}}\mathbb{R}
. In this section, we show that the quasi-parabolic structures corresponding to other invariant subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
correspond to actions of
G
on subspaces of
quotients
of the Heintze group
\mathbb{C}^{n}\rtimes\mathbb{R}
. The proofs in this section are almost identical to those in the previous section, and we simply note where there are differences.

Consider a
\gamma
–invariant subspace
V
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The complexification
W
of
V
is a subspace of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
invariant under
\gamma
and containing
V
. The matrix
\gamma
induces a linear automorphism
\overline{\gamma}
of the (real) quotient vector space
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
. The complexification of
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
is the (complex) quotient vector space
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
, and the action of
\overline{\gamma}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
extends to a linear automorphism of
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
. Choosing a basis for
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
yields a representative of
\overline{\gamma}
as a real matrix. The Jordan normal form of
\overline{\gamma}
is obtained by taking sub-blocks of the Jordan blocks of
\gamma
, and so, as before, it follows that
\overline{\gamma}
is expanding. Unlike the case of
V=0
discussed in the previous section, the matrix
\overline{\gamma}
may not be integral with respect to a particular basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
. However, this will not cause any problems in what follows.

Choose a (real-valued) inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
, and extend it to a complex-valued inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
. Thus, the notion of a unitary matrix acting on
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
is well-defined. Note that any real matrix acting on
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
preserves
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
. We first show that
\overline{\gamma}
lies on a particular one-parameter subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}(\mathbb{C}^{n}/W)
.

Lemma 10.9
.

There is a one-parameter subgroup
\overline{\gamma}^{t}
of
\operatorname{GL}(\mathbb{C}^{n}/W)
of the form
\overline{\gamma}^{t}=\overline{\delta}^{t}\overline{\epsilon}^{t}
where
\overline{\delta}^{t}
is a one-parameter group conjugate into the subgroup of unitary matrices, which commutes with the one-parameter group of real matrices
\overline{\epsilon}^{t}
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Lemma
10.2
, which did not rely on
\gamma
being integral.
∎

Form the Heintze group
(\mathbb{C}^{n}/W)\rtimes_{\overline{\gamma}^{t}}\mathbb{R}
, and equip it with the left-invariant metric induced by the real-valued inner product
\langle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\rangle
on the tangent space
(\mathbb{C}^{n}/W)\times\mathbb{R}
at
(0,0)
defined by
\langle\langle(v,s),(w,t)\rangle\rangle\vcentcolon=\langle\operatorname{Re}v,\operatorname{Re}w\rangle+\langle\operatorname{Im}v,\operatorname{Im}w\rangle+st
. The natural homomorphisms
A\to\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
and
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
induce a homomorphism
G=A\rtimes_{\gamma}\mathbb{Z}\to(\mathbb{C}^{n}/W)\rtimes_{\overline{\gamma}^{t}}\mathbb{R}
, so that
G
acts on this Heintze group isometrically by left translations. Denote the homomorphism
A\to\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
by
\pi
.

Define the subspace
\overline{P}\vcentcolon=(\mathbb{R}^{n}/V)\times\mathbb{R}
of the Lie group
(\mathbb{C}^{n}/W)\rtimes_{\overline{\gamma}^{t}}\mathbb{R}
, which is preserved by
G
.

Proposition 10.10
.

The subspace
\overline{P}
is quasi-isometrically embedded, and hence quasi-convex. Further, the action
G\curvearrowright\overline{P}
is cobounded.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
is the complexification of the real vector space
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
, there is a linear map
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W\to\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
defined by
z\mapsto\operatorname{Re}z
. This defines a retraction
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W\to P
given by
(z,t)\mapsto(\operatorname{Re}z,t)
. The fact that this map is Lipschitz follows from Lemma
10.9
just as Proposition
10.6
follows from Lemma
10.2
.

To prove coboundedness, note that since
A
is dense in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the orbit of
A
is dense in
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V\times\{0\}
. The rest of the proof follows exactly as in Lemma
10.7
. In particular, the orbit of
G
is 2-dense.
∎

Proposition
10.10
implies that
\overline{P}
is a hyperbolic metric space, as quasi-convex subspaces of hyperbolic spaces are hyperbolic (
[
14
, Theorem H.1.9]
). We are now ready to show the association between classes in
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
and actions on Heintze groups.

Lemma 10.11
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, we have
(G\curvearrowright\overline{P})\sim(G\curvearrowright\Gamma(G,Q_{\epsilon}(V)\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}))
.

Proof.

Let
b=(0,0)
be the basepoint of
\overline{P}
. Let
S^{\prime}=\{g\in G:d(b,gb)\leq 5\}
, so that
G\curvearrowright\overline{P}\sim G\curvearrowright\Gamma(G,S^{\prime})
by the Schwarz-Milnor Lemma (Lemma
2.6
). We consider two norms on
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
. The first is the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\operatorname{Euc}}
induced by the inner product that we chose on
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
at the beginning of this subsection. The second is the quotient norm
\|\cdot\|_{\operatorname{Quo}}
induced by the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, i.e.,
\|x\|_{\operatorname{Quo}}=d(x,W)
. Since any norms on a finite-dimensional vector space are equivalent, there is a number
F\geq 1
such that

\frac{1}{F}\|\cdot\|_{\operatorname{Quo}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\operatorname{Euc}}\leq F\|\cdot\|_{\operatorname{Quo}}.

Consider an element
g=xt^{j}\in S^{\prime}\subset G
with
x\in A
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
g\cdot b=(\pi(x),j)
, where
\pi
is the homomorphism
A\to\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
. The first part of the proof of Lemma
10.8
yields a number
L^{\prime}>0
such that
\|\pi(x)\|_{\operatorname{Euc}}\leq 5L^{\prime}
and
|j|\leq 5L^{\prime}
. Thus
\|\pi(x)\|_{\operatorname{Quo}}\leq 5L^{\prime}F
, and
x
lies in the
5L^{\prime}F
-neighborhood of
W
. Since
x
is real, its orthogonal projection to
W
lies in
W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}=V
, and
x\in Q_{5L^{\prime}F}(V)
. Therefore
Q_{5L^{\prime}F}(V)\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}\preceq S^{\prime}
.

On the other hand,
t\in S^{\prime}
. The distance from any element of
Q_{\epsilon}(V)
to
V
is uniformly bounded in terms of
\epsilon
, and the bound approaches 0 as
\epsilon\to 0.
Choose
\eta
small enough that every point of
Q_{\eta}(V)
has distance
\leq 5/F
from
V
. The second part of the proof of Lemma
10.8
shows that for any
x\in Q_{\eta}(V)
,
d(b,xb)\leq\|\pi(x)\|_{\operatorname{Euc}}\leq F\|\pi(x)\|_{\operatorname{Quo}}\leq 5
. Thus
x\in S^{\prime}
as well. This shows that
S^{\prime}\preceq Q_{\eta}(V)\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}
, and the result now follows from Lemma
9.8
.
∎

As
\mathbb{R}^{n}/V
and
\mathbb{C}^{n}/W
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbb{C}^{k}
, respectively, for some
k\leq n
, Theorem
10.1
follows immediately from Lemma
10.11
.

10.3
Proof of Theorems
1.2

and
1.8

In this section we prove the remaining main results from the introduction.

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

Consider an expanding matrix
\gamma\in M_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
with equal minimal and characteristic polynomial, and consider the group
G=G(\gamma)
. By Proposition
4.1
and Lemma
8.1
,
\mathcal{H}(G)
consists of the single elliptic structure plus two lattices
\mathcal{P}_{+}(G)
and
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
meeting in the single lineal structure. By Proposition
9.1
,
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
is isomorphic to the poset of invariant subspaces.

Consider an element of
\mathcal{P}_{-}(G)
. It has the form
[Q_{1}(V)\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}]
for an invariant subspace
V
of
\gamma
. By the discussion in Section
10.2
and Lemma
10.11
, the hyperbolic structure
[Q_{1}(V)\cup\{t^{\pm 1}\}]
is represented by an action on the subset
(\mathbb{R}^{n}/V)\times\mathbb{R}
of a Heintze group
(\mathbb{C}^{n}/W)\rtimes_{\overline{\gamma}^{t}}\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Theorem
1.2
now follows immediately from Theorems
1.8
and
8.7
.

Appendix A
Appendix: Classification of ideals and factorization of formal power series

Co-authored with Sam Payne

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem, which is used to prove Theorem
8.7
.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
f
be a monic polynomial in
\mathbb{Z}[x]
with constant term not lying in
\{-1,0,1\}
. Then the
(x+(f))
-adic completion of
\mathbb{Z}[x]/(f)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
. Moreover, every ideal of the localization
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
is generated by a divisor of
f
in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. The poset of ideals of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
is isomorphic to the poset of divisors of
f
. Equivalently, the poset of ideals of
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
considered up to equivalence is isomorphic to the poset of divisors of
f
.

Consider the ring
R=\mathbb{Z}[x]/(f)
and its element
\gamma=x+(f)
. First we give an alternative description of the
(\gamma)
-adic completion
\widehat{R}
of
R
, with no assumptions on
f
.

Lemma A.2
.

The
(\gamma)
-adic completion of
\mathbb{Z}[x]/(f)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
.

Proof.

The sequence

0\to(f)\to\mathbb{Z}[x]\to R\to 0

is an exact sequence of finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}[x]
-modules. Since
\mathbb{Z}[x]
is Noetherian, the sequence of
(x)
–adic completions

0\to\widehat{(f)}\to\widehat{\mathbb{Z}[x]}\to\widehat{R}\to 0

is exact by
[
6
, Proposition 10.12]
. Since
x
acts on
R
in the same way as
\gamma
, the
(x)
-adic completion
\widehat{R}
is just the usual
(\gamma)
-adic completion. Moreover,
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}[x]}
is the formal power series ring
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. The
\mathbb{Z}[x]
-module homomorphism
(f)\to\widehat{(f)}
induces a
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
-module isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[x]}(f)\to\widehat{(f)}
by
[
6
, Proposition 10.13]
, whose image is the ideal of
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
generated by
f
.
∎

For the rest of the section, we consider quotients of
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
by monic polynomials with non-zero constant terms that are not units in
\mathbb{Z}
. To prove Theorem
A.1
, we must classify the ideals of
x^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f))
. Note that localization commutes with taking quotients (
[
6
, Corollary 3.4(iii)]
). Therefore
x^{-1}(\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f))
is canonically identified with the quotient of the Laurent series ring
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
by its principal ideal
(f)
, and we denote this ring by
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
.

Recall that elements
a
and
b
in a ring are
associates
if
b=ua
for some unit
u\in R
. We first consider quotients
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g)
, where
g
is an irreducible power series that is neither associate to any prime in
\mathbb{Z}
nor to the monomial
x
. Thus the constant term of
g
is equal to a power of a prime
p\in\mathbb{Z}
times
\pm 1
. By
[
28
, Proposition 3.1.3]
(see also
[
18
, Theorem 1.4]
), there is an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g)\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\alpha]
sending
x
to
\alpha
, where

\alpha
is a root of an irreducible polynomial
w(x)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]
such that

w(x)=pu(x)+x^{n}\text{ where }u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]\text{ and }\deg(u)\leq n-1.

(4)

Lemma A.3
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
be an irreducible power series that is neither associate to
x
nor to any prime in
\mathbb{Z}
. Then
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g)
is a field.

Proof.

Consider the prime
p\in\mathbb{Z}
coming from the constant term of
g
and the polynomial
w\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]
as in (
4
). Then
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g)
is isomorphic to
\alpha^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\alpha]
. Since
\alpha^{n}=-pu(\alpha)
, it follows that
p
is a unit in
\alpha^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\alpha]
. There is a natural injection from
\alpha^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\alpha]
to the finite field extension
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\alpha)
:
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\alpha)=\mathbb{Q}_{p}[x]/(w)
, and
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]/(w)
includes into
\mathbb{Q}_{p}[x]/(w)
in such a way that
x+(w)
is a unit. We claim that this homomorphism is also surjective and thus an isomorphism of fields. We may write an element of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\alpha)
as
h(\alpha)
with
h\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}[x]
. Multiplying by a high enough power of
p
to clear the denominators of the coefficients of
h
, we have
p^{i}h(x)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[x]
for some
i
. Since
p
is a unit in
\alpha^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\alpha]
, we have
p^{-i}(p^{i}h(\alpha))\in\alpha^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\alpha]
, and the image of this element is
h(\alpha)
. Thus, the natural map
\alpha^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\alpha]\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\alpha)
is surjective as desired.
∎

Next, we consider quotients by powers of irreducibles
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g^{j})
, where
g
is neither associate to
x
nor to any prime
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\bar{h}\vcentcolon=h+(g^{j})
denote the image of a power series
h\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
in this quotient.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
be an irreducible power series that is neither associate to
x
nor to any prime in
\mathbb{Z}
. If
j\geq 2
, then
(g)
is the unique non-zero prime ideal of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g^{j})
. Moreover, the ideals of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g^{j})
are exactly the ideals
({g}^{i})
generated by powers
{g}^{i}
for
0\leq i\leq j
.

Proof.

The quotient of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g^{j})
by
(g)
is a field by Lemma
A.3
. Thus
(g)
is maximal, and hence prime. Moreover, we have
(g)^{j}=0
. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a non-zero prime ideal of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g^{j})
. Then
(g)^{j}\subset\mathfrak{p}
, and, since
\mathfrak{p}
is prime, we must have
(g)\subset\mathfrak{p}
. As
(g)
is maximal, it follows that
\mathfrak{p}=(g)
. Thus
(g)
is the unique prime ideal of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g^{j})
. This proves the first statement.

For the moreover statement, note that any element of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g^{j})
can be written as
\bar{h}/\bar{x}^{k}
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. If
g
does not divide
h
, then
\bar{h}/\bar{x}^{k}
is a unit. To see this, suppose for contradiction that
\bar{h}/\bar{x}^{k}\in(g)
. Then
\bar{h}/\bar{x}^{k}=\bar{g}\bar{h^{\prime}}/\bar{x}^{\ell}
for some
h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and so
\bar{h}\bar{x}^{\ell}=\bar{g}\bar{h^{\prime}}\bar{x}^{k}
. In other words,
hx^{\ell}+(g^{j})=gh^{\prime}x^{k}+(g^{j})
so that
hx^{\ell}=gh^{\prime}x^{k}+g^{j}h^{\prime\prime}
for some
h^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. However,
g
divides the right hand side of this equation while it does not divide the left, which is a contradiction. Thus, if
g
does not divide
h
then
\bar{h}/\bar{x}^{k}
does not lie in
(g)
, so it is a unit.

Given any
\bar{h}/\bar{x}^{k}\in x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g^{j})
, we may write
h=h^{\prime}g^{i}
, where
i\geq 0
and
h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
is not divisible by
g
. Then
\bar{h}/\bar{x}^{k}=(\bar{h^{\prime}}/\bar{x}^{k})(\bar{g}^{i}/1)
, and, by the previous paragraph,
\bar{h^{\prime}}/\bar{x}^{k}
is a unit. In other words,
\bar{h}/\bar{x}^{k}
is associate to
\bar{g}^{i}
. Consider an ideal
\mathfrak{a}
, and choose
i
to be the minimum such that there is an element of
\mathfrak{a}
associate to
\bar{g}^{i}
. Then
\mathfrak{a}\subset(\bar{g}^{i})
, but also
\bar{g}^{i}\in\mathfrak{a}
. That is,
\mathfrak{a}=(\bar{g}^{i})=(\bar{g})^{i}
.
∎

A localization of a unique factorization domain is a unique factorization domain. Thus
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
is a unique factorization domain, and so its prime and irreducible elements coincide.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
be a prime power series which is neither associate to
x
nor to a prime
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
g
is prime as an element of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. Moreover, consider two prime power series
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
, neither of which is associate to
x
or to a prime
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are not associate to each other in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
, then
g_{1}^{n_{1}}
and
g_{2}^{n_{2}}
are coprime in
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
for any
n_{1},n_{2}\geq 1
.

Proof.

If
g
is a unit in
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
, then there is an element
h/x^{k}\in x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
with
gh/x^{k}=1
in
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. Hence
gh=x^{k}
. However, this contradicts that
g
does not divide the prime power
x^{k}
, and so
g
is not a unit.

Consider a product of two elements of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
that is equal to
g
; that is,
g=(h_{1}/x^{k_{1}})(h_{2}/x^{k_{2}})
where
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. Then
gx^{k_{1}}x^{k_{2}}=h_{1}h_{2}
in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
, and so
g
divides exactly one of the
h_{i}
. Say
g
divides
h_{1}
. Then
g
and
h_{1}/x^{k_{1}}
divide each other in the domain
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
, and therefore they are associates while
h_{2}/x^{k_{2}}
is a unit. This proves that
g
is irreducible in
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
and hence also prime.

For the last statement, consider two non-associate prime power series
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
with the desired properties. By the proof of Lemma
A.4
, an element of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g_{2}^{n_{2}})
is either a unit or nilpotent. Moreover, an element
\overline{h}/\overline{x}^{k}
with
h\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
is nilpotent exactly if
g_{2}
divides
h
in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. The element
\overline{g_{1}}^{n_{1}}\in x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(g_{2}^{n_{2}})
is a unit, since
g_{2}
does not divide
g_{1}^{n_{1}}
in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. Thus,
\overline{g_{1}}^{n_{1}}(\overline{h}/\overline{x}^{k})=\overline{1}
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. In other words,
g_{1}^{n_{1}}h+(g_{2}^{n_{2}})=x^{k}+(g_{2}^{n_{2}})
, and hence
g_{1}^{n_{1}}h=x^{k}+g_{2}^{n_{2}}h^{\prime}
for some
h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]].
Therefore
1=g_{1}^{n_{1}}(h/x^{k})-g_{2}^{n_{2}}(h^{\prime}/x^{k})\in(g_{1}^{n_{1}},g_{2}^{n_{2}})
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
A.1
.

Proof of Theorem
A.1
.

Consider a monic polynomial
f\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
whose constant term does not lie in
\{-1,0,1\}
and its prime factorization
f=uf_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots f_{r}^{n_{r}}
in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
, where
u\in\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
is a unit and each
f_{i}
is prime. Since the constant term of
f
is not
\pm 1
, the polynomial
f
is not a unit in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. Thus there is at least one prime power series in its prime factorization. The prime power series of
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
are either associate to
x
, associate to a prime in
\mathbb{Z}
, or neither associate to
x
nor to any prime in
\mathbb{Z}
. A power series of the last type has constant term equal to a power of a prime in
\mathbb{Z}
times
\pm 1
.
A prime of the first type has constant term 0, while a prime of the second type has all its coefficients divisible by
p
. Since
f
has non-zero constant term, the same holds for each
f_{i}
, and so no
f_{i}
is associate to
x
. As
f
is monic, the coefficients of
f
are not all divisible by a common prime integer
p
, and so the same holds for each
f_{i}
, and no
f_{i}
is associate to a prime integer
p
. In particular, this implies that the constant term of each
f_{i}
is a power of a prime in
\mathbb{Z}
times
\pm 1
.

By Theorem
6.17
, the poset of ideals of
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
considered up to multiplication by
\gamma=x+(f)
is isomorphic to the poset of ideals of the localization
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
. We have
(f)=(f_{1}^{n_{1}})\cdots(f_{r}^{n_{r}})
in
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
, and by Lemma
A.5
, the ideals
(f_{i}^{n_{i}})
are pairwise coprime. Thus, by the Chinese Remainder Theorem,

x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)\cong x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f_{1}^{n_{1}})\times\cdots\times x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f_{r}^{n_{r}}).

An ideal of this ring has the form
\mathfrak{a}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{a}_{r}
, where each
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is an ideal of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f_{i}^{n_{i}})
. By Lemma
A.4
, an ideal must actually have the form
(f_{1}^{i_{1}})\times\cdots\times(f_{r}^{i_{r}})
, where
0\leq i_{j}\leq n_{j}
for each
j
.

The divisors of
f
in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
up to associates are
f_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots f_{r}^{i_{r}}
, and the map

f_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots f_{r}^{i_{r}}\mapsto(f_{1}^{i_{1}})\times\cdots\times(f_{r}^{i_{r}})

is a bijection from the poset of divisors of
f
with the order of divisibility to the poset of ideals of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
. This bijection is order-reversing, and thus the poset of ideals of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
is isomorphic to the opposite of the poset of divisors of
f
in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
. The poset of divisors of
f
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Div}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{r})
, which is isomorphic to its own opposite. Therefore, the poset of ideals of
x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[[x]]/(f)
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Div}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{r})
and to the poset of divisors of
f
in
\mathbb{Z}[[x]]
.
∎

Appendix B
Appendix: Pseudo-valuations and confining subsets

The definition of a confining subset in this paper (Definition
2.10
) is only for groups with abelianizations of rank 1.
Nonetheless, groups of the more general form
H\rtimes\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, for
H
an abelian group and
n\geq 1
, were considered by the authors in
[
2
]
, in which an analogous concept of confining subsets was defined and some of the results of
[
16
]
were generalized.
In this section, we work in this more general setting and introduce
pseudo-valuations
, which are functions that are a slight weakening of valuations.
The main result of this section is the following; the terms in the statement are defined immediately after.

Theorem B.1
.

Let
G=H\rtimes_{\alpha}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
where
H
is abelian. Given a homomorphism
\rho\colon\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, the poset of equivalence classes of pseudo-valuations on
H
subordinate to
\rho
is isomorphic to the opposite of the poset of equivalence classes of subsets of
H
which are confining under
\alpha
with respect to
\rho
.

We start by giving the general definition of a confining subset and then define pseudo-valuations subordinate to
\rho
. Let
H
be abelian, and let
G=H\rtimes_{\alpha}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, where
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\operatorname{Aut}(H)
is a fixed homomorphism. An element
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
acts on
H
by conjugation via
zhz^{-1}=\alpha(z)(h)
. Fix a (non-zero) homomorphism
\rho\colon\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Definition B.2
.

A symmetric subset
Q
of
H
is
confining under
\alpha
with respect to
\rho
if the following hold:

(a)

For all
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\rho(z)\geq 0
,
\alpha(z)(Q)\subseteq Q
.

(b)

For each
h\in H
, there exists
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
\alpha(z)(h)\in Q
.

(c)

There exists
z_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
\alpha(z_{0})(Q+Q)\subseteq Q
.

If there exists
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\rho(z)>0
such that
\alpha(z)(Q)\subsetneq Q
, then
Q
is
strictly confining
.

Definition B.3
.

A
pseudo-valuation subordinate to
\rho
is a function
\widetilde{v}\colon H\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
satisfying:

(PV1)

there exists a constant
\lambda\geq 0
such that
\widetilde{v}(h_{1}+h_{2})\geq\operatorname{min}\{\widetilde{v}(h_{1}),\widetilde{v}(h_{2})\}-\lambda
for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
;

(PV2)

\widetilde{v}(h)=\widetilde{v}(-h)
for all
h\in H
; and

(PV3)

\widetilde{v}(\alpha(z)(h))=\rho(z)+\widetilde{v}(h)
for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
h\in H
.

Remark B.4
.

Since the image of
\rho
is unbounded in
\mathbb{R}
and
\widetilde{v}(\alpha(z)(0))=\widetilde{v}(0)
for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, it follows from condition (PV3) above that
\widetilde{v}(0)=+\infty
.

There is an equivalence relation on the set of homomorphisms
\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
given by
\rho_{1}\sim\rho_{2}
if and only if
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are positive scalar multiples of each other.

Fix a generating set
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. We construct a (possibly infinite) generating set of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
as follows. Fix a constant
C_{\rho}>0
such that
\rho(t_{i})\in[-C_{\rho},C_{\rho}]
for all
i\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and there exists
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
\rho(y)=C_{\rho}
, and let

Z_{\rho}=\{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}:|\rho(z)|\leq C_{\rho}\}.

(5)

Suppose that
Q\subseteq H
is confining under
\alpha
with respect to
\rho
. It is straightforward to check that
Q\cup Z_{\rho}
is symmetric, and
Q\cup Z_{\rho}
generates
G
by Definition
B.2
(b). We denote the word norm on
G
with respect to
Q\cup Z_{\rho}
by
\|\cdot\|_{Q\cup Z_{\rho}}
. If
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are two confining subsets with respect to
\rho
, then we say that
Q_{1}\preceq_{\rho}Q_{2}
if
\displaystyle\operatorname{sup}_{q_{2}\in Q_{2}}\|q_{2}\|_{Q_{1}\cup Z_{\rho}}<\infty
or, equivalently,
[Q_{1}\cup Z_{\rho}]\preccurlyeq[Q_{2}\cup Z_{\rho}]
as generating sets. The confining subsets are equivalent, denoted
Q_{1}\sim_{\rho}Q_{2}
, if
Q_{1}\preceq_{\rho}Q_{2}
and
Q_{2}\preceq_{\rho}Q_{1}
.

We also consider an equivalence relation on pseudo-valuations.

Definition B.5
.

Suppose that
\widetilde{v},\widetilde{u}
are two pseudo-valuations on
H
subordinate to
\rho
. We say that
\widetilde{v}\preceq_{\rho}\widetilde{u}
if there exists a constant
K\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\widetilde{v}(h)\leq\tilde{u}(h)+K
for all
h\in H
. The preorder
\preceq_{\rho}
induces an equivalence relation
\sim_{\rho}
as usual via
\widetilde{v}\sim_{\rho}\widetilde{u}
if
\widetilde{v}\preceq_{\rho}\widetilde{u}
and
\widetilde{u}\preceq_{\rho}\widetilde{v}
.

The first result of this section describes how to obtain a confining subset from a pseudo-valuation.
Define a map
\operatorname{Conf}
from the set of pseudo-valuations subordinate to
\rho
to subsets of
H
as follows:

\operatorname{Conf}(\widetilde{v})=\{h\in H:\widetilde{v}(h)\geq 0\}.

(6)

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\widetilde{v}
be a pseudo-valuation subordinate to
\rho
.
Then
\operatorname{Conf}(\widetilde{v})
is symmetric and confining under
\alpha
with respect to
\rho
.

Proof.

Set
Q=\operatorname{Conf}(\widetilde{v})
. Note that (PV2) ensures that
Q
is symmetric.
We verify the conditions of Definition
B.2
. Let
q\in Q
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be such that
\rho(z)\geq 0
. Then
\widetilde{v}(q)\geq 0
, and thus
\widetilde{v}(\alpha(z)(q))=\rho(z)+\widetilde{v}(q)\geq 0
and
\alpha(z)(q)\in Q
. This verifies Definition
B.2
(a).
To show Definition
B.2
(b) holds, let
h\in H
. By choosing
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
\rho(z)+\widetilde{v}(h)\geq 0
, we ensure that
\widetilde{v}(\alpha(z)(h))\geq 0
. Thus
\alpha(z)(h)\in Q
, as desired.
For Definition
B.2
(c), it follows from (PV3) that
\widetilde{v}
is at least
-\lambda
on
Q+Q
. Taking
z_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\rho(z_{0})\geq\lambda
yields
\alpha(z_{0})(Q+Q)\subset Q
.
∎

Conversely, define a map
\operatorname{Pval}
from subsets of
H
which are confining under
\alpha
with respect to
\rho
to the set of functions
H\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
by

\operatorname{Pval}(Q)(h)=-\operatorname{inf}\{\rho(z):z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\textrm{ and }\alpha(z)(h)\in Q\}.

(7)

Lemma B.7
.

Let
Q\subseteq H
be a subset of
H
which is confining under
\alpha
with respect to
\rho
. Then
\operatorname{Pval}(Q)
is a pseudo-valuation subordinate to
\rho
.

Proof.

Let
\operatorname{Pval}(Q)=\widetilde{v}
. Note that
\widetilde{v}
always takes values in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
, as
\widetilde{v}(h)=-\infty
is impossible by Definition
B.2
(b). We verify the conditions from Definition
B.3
.

Condition (PV1) is equivalent to the existence of
\lambda\geq 0
such that the following holds for every
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
:

\operatorname{inf}\{\rho(z):\alpha(z)(h_{1}+h_{2})\in Q\}\leq\operatorname{max}\{i_{1},i_{2}\}+\lambda,

where
i_{j}=\operatorname{inf}\{\rho(z):\alpha(z)(h_{j})\in Q\}
for
j=1,2
. We may assume, without loss of generality, that
i_{1}\leq i_{2}
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be such that
\alpha(z)(h_{2})\in Q
. As
i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\rho(z)
, we also have
\alpha(z)(h_{1})\in Q
.
Thus
\alpha(z)(h_{1}+h_{2})\in Q+Q
. Let
z_{0}
be as in Definition
B.2
(c). We may assume
\rho(z_{0})\geq 0
, and so
\alpha(z_{0}z)(h_{1}+h_{2})\in Q
. Thus

\operatorname{inf}\{\rho(z):\alpha(z)(h_{1}+h_{2})\in Q\}\leq\rho(z_{0}z)=\rho(z)+\rho(z_{0}).

Setting
\lambda=\rho(z_{0})
and taking the infimum over all
z
such that
\alpha(z)(h_{2})\in Q
, we obtain
\operatorname{inf}\{\rho(z):\alpha(z)(h_{1}+h_{2})\in Q\}\leq i_{2}+\lambda
, establishing (PV1).

To check condition (PV2), note that
\alpha(z)(h)\in Q
if and only if
\alpha(z)(-h)\in Q
since
Q
is symmetric. This shows that
\widetilde{v}(h)=\widetilde{v}(-h)
.

Finally, for a fixed
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
h\in H
, we have that

\widetilde{v}(\alpha(z)(h))=-\operatorname{inf}\{\rho(z^{\prime}):\alpha(z^{\prime})(\alpha(z)(h))\in Q\}=-\operatorname{inf}\{\rho(z^{\prime}):\alpha(z^{\prime}z)(h))\in Q\}.

Let
z^{\prime}z=y
. Then
z^{\prime}=yz^{-1}
, and

\operatorname{inf}\{\rho(z^{\prime}):\alpha(z^{\prime}z)(h)\in Q\}=\operatorname{inf}\{\rho(y)-\rho(z):\alpha(y)(h)\in Q\}=\operatorname{inf}\{\rho(y):\alpha(y)(h)\in Q\}-\rho(z).

Taking the negative infimum yields
\widetilde{v}(\alpha(z)(h))=\widetilde{v}(h)+\rho(z)
, which proves condition (PV3).
∎

Thus far, we have a map
\operatorname{Conf}
from pseudo-valuations subordinate to
\rho
to confining subsets with respect to
\rho
, and a map
\operatorname{Pval}
in the opposite direction. Since we have equivalence relations on the confining subsets and on pseudo-valuations, our next goal is to ensure that these maps descend to the equivalence classes. We first show that
\operatorname{Pval}
is order-reversing, and as a consequence, respects equivalence. For this and other results proved in this section, we utilize the following alternative characterization of the order on confining subsets.

Lemma B.8
.

Suppose that
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are confining subsets with respect to
\rho
. Then
Q_{2}\preceq_{\rho}Q_{1}
if and only if there exists
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\alpha(z)(Q_{1})\subset Q_{2}
for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
\rho(z)\geq\mu
.

Proof.

We first show the forward implication. Let
q\in Q_{1}
. Since
Q_{2}\preceq_{\rho}Q_{1}
, there is a constant
K_{1}
such that
\|q\|_{Q_{2}\cup Z_{\rho}}\leq K_{1}
for all
q\in Q_{1}
. It follows from
[
2
, Lemma 3.6]
that there is a geodesic representative for
q
in
Q_{2}\cup Z_{\rho}
of the form
w^{-1}s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{m}w
for some
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, some
s_{i}\in Q_{2}
, and some
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\|w\|_{Q_{2}\cup Z_{\rho}}\leq K_{1}
and
m\leq K_{1}
. By the definition of
Z_{\rho}
,
|\rho(w)|\leq K_{1}C_{\rho}
.

Since
wqw^{-1}=s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{m}\in Q^{K_{1}}_{2}
, we have
\alpha(w)(q)\in Q^{K_{1}}_{2}
. Letting
z_{0}
be the element from Definition
B.2
(c) for
Q_{2}
, we see that
\alpha(z^{K_{1}}_{0}w)(q)\in\alpha(z^{K_{1}}_{0})(Q^{K_{1}}_{2})\subset Q_{2}.

We claim that the forward implication holds for
\mu=K_{1}\rho(z_{0})+K_{1}C_{\rho}\in\mathbb{R}
. Indeed, if
\rho(z)\geq\mu
, then
\rho(zz_{0}^{-K_{1}}w^{-1})\geq 0
,
and so
\alpha(z)(q)=\alpha(zz^{-K_{1}}_{0}w^{-1})\alpha(z^{K_{1}}_{0}w)(q)\in\alpha(zz^{-K_{1}}_{0}w^{-1})(Q_{2})\subset Q_{2}
.

For the reverse implication, suppose that we have a
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\alpha(z)(Q_{1})\subset Q_{2}
for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
satisfying
\rho(z)\geq\mu
. Fix a
z_{1}
such that
\rho(z_{1})\geq\mu
. Then
\|q\|_{Q_{2}\cup Z_{\rho}}\leq 1+2\|z_{1}\|_{Z_{\rho}}
for any
q\in Q_{1}
, giving a uniform bound independent of
q
. ∎

Proposition B.9
.

Suppose that
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are confining subsets with respect to
\rho
such that
Q_{2}\preceq_{\rho}Q_{1}
. Then
\operatorname{Pval}(Q_{1})\preceq_{\rho}\operatorname{Pval}(Q_{2})
. In particular, if
Q_{2}\sim_{\rho}Q_{1}
, then
\operatorname{Pval}(Q_{2})\sim_{\rho}\operatorname{Pval}(Q_{1})
.

Proof.

Set
